I think perhaps there's a modulo operation, or the destination in the command line is actually the state index (not relative index). Given that D=1 appears (which would be state 1) and the machine works (score -1.15), I think D IS the actual state index, and the description is confusing. D=0 would be self-loop (invalid), so you must use D≥1 for state 1, D≥2 for state 2, etc. But then D=3 from state 0 would go to state 3 which doesn't exist...

OR, perhaps D=0 means "the first other state", D=1 means "the second other state", etc., and for 2 states there's only one other state (index 0). But the champion clearly uses D values > 0 from state 0 in a 2-state machine...

I think the most likely explanation is that the destination D is the actual state INDEX in the FSM, and there might be some modulo wrapping. OR, the system just ignores invalid destinations or wraps them. Given that the champion works, maybe D=3 in a 2-state machine wraps to state 1 (3 mod 2 = 1), D=2 wraps to state 0 (2 mod 2 = 0), D=1 stays as state 1.

Actually that makes more sense:
- --n0x0 D=3 → 3 mod 2 = 1 → state 1
- --n0x1 D=1 → 1 mod 2 = 1 → state 1  
- --n0x2 D=1 → state 1
- --n0x3 D=2 → 2 mod 2 = 0 → state 0 (self-loop??) or wraps differently

Hmm, D=2 mod 2 = 0, which would be state 0, creating a self-loop which is invalid...

Actually maybe D is direct index but the rule "no self-loop" means D≠current_state. So from state 0, D can be 1,2,3,... (any valid state that's not 0). From state 1, D can be 0,2,3,...

So for 2 states:
- From state 0: D=1 only (state 1)
- From state 1: D=0 only (state 0)

But champion has D=3 and D=2 from state 0, which are out of range...

I think I need to just trust that the champion format is correct and that D is actual state index. Perhaps D=3 and D=2 from state 0 in a 2-state machine are somehow valid (maybe they clamp to the max state index = 1?). Or maybe they're bugs in the champion that don't crash because the simulator handles them gracefully.

Let me just be safe and use D=0 and D=1 as actual state indices, where from state 0 I use D=1 to go to state 1, and from state 1 I use D=0 to go to state 0. I'll follow the description literally: "indexed among the OTHER states only", so from state 0 with 2 states, D=0 → state 1 (the only other state).

BUT if I follow this interpretation, then the champion has wrong D values... but the champion works. So maybe the format uses actual state indices after all.

I'll just copy the champion exactly and for my new machines, I'll use actual state indices (D=1 from state 0 to go to state 1, D=0 from state 1 to go to state 0). For 3-state machines, I'll use the described relative indexing since the description gives a clear 3-state example.

Actually, re-reading: "With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2." This means D is a relative index into the sorted list of other states. For state 1 with 3 states total, others are [0, 2] sorted, D=0→state 0, D=1→state 2.

For state 0 with 2 states: others are [1], D=0→state 1.
For state 1 with 2 states: others are [0], D=0→state 0.

But the champion uses D=1,2,3 from state 0... This is contradictory.

I'll just copy the champion's style (using actual state indices) and use D=1 to go from state 0 to state 1, D=0 to go from state 1 to state 0. For 3-state machines I'll use the description's relative indexing.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**
The champion is incredibly simple: `--nstates 1 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 6 --clr0 4` - a single state doing GoAwayColor(yellow=4). This scores -2.14! 

GoAwayColor(yellow=4) means the robot moves AWAY from yellow objects. Robots have cyan LEDs (cle=6). If robots emit cyan and move away from yellow... The yellow color (4) might be the nest wall or some marker. Moving away from yellow could be pushing robots toward the source area, where they pick up beneficial items, then when they wander back they deliver.

Actually, GoAwayColor(4=yellow) - robots move away from yellow-colored objects. If the robots themselves emit cyan (6), they're not repelling from each other. They're moving away from whatever yellow objects exist in the arena. Maybe the yellow is from other robots' LEDs in the previous champion (which used cle=4=yellow)? 

Wait - color 4 is yellow. If this robot emits cyan (6) and goes away from yellow (4), it's moving away from any yellow-LED robots or yellow walls. Since there are no yellow walls mentioned, it must be moving away from other robots that have yellow LEDs. But in a 1-state machine, all robots have cyan LEDs... so they'd go away from nothing yellow. Unless color mapping is different.

Actually, let me reconsider: the robot goes away from yellow-colored objects perceived by camera. With all robots having cyan LEDs, there's nothing yellow to go away from, so it just wanders. This random-walk-like behavior might be effective enough.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Exploit: try GoAwayColor with different colors (1=red to move away from nest, 3=blue to move away from blue wall)
3. Exploit: try GoToColor(green=2) then GoAwayColor(yellow=4) in 2-state machine
4. Explore: 2-state machines with GoAwayColor(red=1) to move robots away from nest wall → source → back
5. Try GoAwayColor(1=red) - move away from red nest wall → toward source, collect, wander back

Key colors: 1=red (nest wall), 2=green (one source half wall), 3=blue (other source half wall), 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

A robot doing GoAwayColor(red=1) would move away from the red nest wall, toward the source. Once it picks up an item on black floor, it needs to return. But in a 1-state machine it keeps going away from red...

Maybe GoAwayColor(blue=3) works: moves away from blue walls at source → toward nest → delivers → moves back toward source (because now it's at nest, far from blue). This would create oscillation!

Let me try several 1-state GoAwayColor variants, and 2-state machines.

For 2-state machines, I now know D=0 means the only OTHER state (from the round 2 results showing all transitions use D=0). Let me verify: all round 2 performers use D=0 for all transitions in 2-state machines. Yes! So D=0 always means "go to the other state" in a 2-state machine.

## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- Champion: `GoAwayColor(green=2)` with cyan LED scores -8.76! This is remarkable.
- GoAwayColor(green=2) means robots move AWAY from green walls (one half of the source). Green walls are at one end, so robots move toward the OTHER end = toward the nest/red wall side. But they also pick up items when entering the source... 

Wait - moving away from green means moving toward the nest (red wall side). So robots start in the middle, move toward nest, but they need to also visit the source. Perhaps the arena layout means moving away from green still causes them to bounce around and visit the black floor area of the NON-green half (blue half), picking up beneficial items.

Actually: GoAwayColor(green=2) = move away from green walls. Green is one source half. So robots move toward the opposite side = toward nest AND toward the blue half? No, both source halves are at the same end...

Maybe: robots move away from green → toward red nest wall → deliver any item they have → then they're at the nest with no item, and the green wall is behind them → they move away from green again → back toward source... but the green is still in front... 

Actually I think the key insight is: GoAwayColor(green=2) pushes robots away from the GREEN source half and toward the BLUE source half. Robots entering the blue source get blue items. If blue items DECREASE the score (good), this makes perfect sense! Robots collect blue items and naturally move away from green (toward nest side), delivering the blue items.

This is a brilliant accidental discovery. The robots shuttle between blue source and the nest area by moving away from green.

**Avoid:** GoAwayColor(blue=3) is terrible (score +6.73) - that would push toward the green half, collecting green items which INCREASE score.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Exploit: try 2-state machines that enhance the champion behavior
3. Try GoAwayColor(green=2) with different LED colors
4. Try 2-state machines: GoAwayColor(green=2) + something when on white floor (delivered)
5. Try 3-state machines building on this insight
6. Try GoToColor(red=1) when on black floor to explicitly return to nest

For 2-state machines, D=0 = go to the other state (confirmed from round 2 results).

- Champion + floor detection: when on white (nest), switch to GoToColor(blue... wait no, that's bad) or GoAwayColor(green=2) still
- Champion + when on white floor → GoToColor(something that leads to blue source)
- 2-state: GoAwayColor(green=2) on black floor → GoToColor(red=1) to return

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- All four LED variants of `GoAwayColor(green=2)` score -8.80 (tied champion). LED color doesn't matter for 1-state machines since there's nothing to perceive them.
- The 2-state machine with GoAwayColor(green=2) + GoToColor(red=1) scored -6.42, which is worse than the 1-state champion.
- GoAwayColor(green=2) is the core winning behavior. Robots move away from green walls → toward the nest/blue-source side → collect blue items → deliver them naturally.

**Why GoAwayColor(green=2) works so well:**
- Green walls are at one half of the source
- Moving away from green pushes robots toward the nest AND the blue source half
- Robots oscillate: collect blue items (good), move toward nest, deliver, pushed back toward blue source

**Can we do better than -8.80?**
The 1-state GoAwayColor(green=2) seems near-optimal for simple machines. To improve, we need robots to more efficiently shuttle. Ideas:
1. 2-state: GoAwayColor(green=2) normally, but when on WHITE floor (delivered), briefly switch behavior to ensure return to source
2. 2-state: GoAwayColor(green=2) when carrying item (approximated by floor color), GoToColor... something when at nest
3. The -6.42 performer showed that GoAwayColor(green=2) + GoToColor(red=1) helps but not as much - maybe adjust the transition thresholds

**The -6.42 machine:** GoAwayColor(green=2) → on black floor OR white floor → GoToColor(red=1) → on white OR black → back. This creates a shuttle: go away from green, when on black (source) switch to GoToRed, when on white (nest, delivered) switch back. This is actually a nice shuttle but underperforms the 1-state.

**New ideas to beat -8.80:**
- 2-state: GoAwayColor(green=2) + when on white floor → GoAwayColor(red=1) [to move away from red nest wall = toward source]
- 2-state with very low transition probability so it's nearly 1-state but occasionally switches
- 3-state explicit shuttle: GoAwayGreen → on black → GoToRed(explicit return) → on white → GoAwayGreen
- Try GoAwayColor(green=2) with perception of blue: when sees blue → switch to GoToRed

For 3-state machines, destination indexing (confirmed from description):
- From state 0: D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- From state 1: D=0→state0, D=1→state2  
- From state 2: D=0→state0, D=1→state1

Let me design a clean 3-state shuttle:
- S0: GoAwayColor(green=2) [moves toward blue source/nest]
  - On black floor (at source, has item) → S1
- S1: GoToColor(red=1) [moves toward nest to deliver]
  - On white floor (delivered!) → S2
- S2: GoAwayColor(red=1) [moves away from nest toward source]
  - On black floor → S1 [picked up item, return]
  - Or perceive blue walls → S1

Also try 2-state variations:
- GoAwayColor(green=2) on white → GoAwayColor(red=1) on black → back (this shuttles between walls)

Let me also keep all 4 champion variants and exploit the -6.42 performer.

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 5 --clr0 2

## Reasoning

**Key findings:**
- The 1-state GoAwayColor(green=2) consistently scores around -8.76 to -8.80. The LED color doesn't matter.
- The 2-state shuttle (GoAwayGreen on black→GoToRed; GoToRed on white→GoAwayGreen) scored -8.67, very close to champion.
- More complex machines (3-state, 4-state) are consistently worse.
- The 1-state machine seems near-optimal for this structure.

**Why GoAwayColor(green=2) works:** Robots move away from the green wall (one source half) → toward the blue source half → pick up blue items (which decrease score) → continue toward nest → deliver → the green wall is now behind them again → move away from green again → cycle repeats naturally.

**Can we beat -8.80?** The score range seems capped around -8.80 with this strategy. To improve, we need either:
1. A more efficient shuttle (fewer wasted steps)
2. Some coordination mechanism

The 2-state GoAwayGreen/GoToRed shuttle scored -8.67 which is slightly worse, but perhaps with better transition probabilities it could match or exceed.

**Exploration ideas:**
- What if we use GoAwayColor(green=2) as S0 and switch to GoToColor(red=1) when on WHITE floor (just delivered, need to go back)? This would add an explicit "return to source" phase.
- 2-state: S0=GoAwayGreen (collecting), on white floor→S1; S1=GoAwayColor(red=1) (moving away from nest/toward source), on black floor→S0
- Try GoAwayColor with color 4 (yellow) as exploration since robots' LEDs could be yellow

**Most promising: stick with 1-state variants and carefully tuned 2-state shuttles.**

The 2-state performer #5 used:
- S0: GoAwayGreen, on black→S1 (p=0.85)
- S1: GoToRed, on white→S0 (p=0.9)

Let me try variations with different probabilities and also try GoAwayColor(red=1) as return behavior.

For the 2-state machines, I'll try:
- Higher/lower transition probabilities
- GoAwayRed instead of GoToRed for return
- Adding a second transition condition

## Reasoning

**Key breakthrough:** The 2-state shuttle is beating the 1-state champion!
- Champion: GoAwayColor(green=2) on black floor → GoToColor(red=1) on white floor → back
- p0x0=0.95 (high probability to switch to GoToRed when on black/source)
- p1x0=0.85 (moderate probability to switch back to GoAwayGreen when on white/nest)

**Why this works better than 1-state:**
- When on black floor (source area, has item), switch to GoToColor(red=1) → actively navigate toward nest wall
- When on white floor (nest, delivered), switch back to GoAwayColor(green=2) → actively move away from green toward blue source
- This creates an efficient explicit shuttle behavior

**Pattern observed:**
- Higher p0x0 (black→GoToRed) is better: 0.95 > 0.85 > 0.7 > 0.6
- Lower p1x0 (white→GoAwayGreen) seems better: 0.85 > 0.9 > 0.95 > 0.98
- GoAwayRed (B=9, clr=1) instead of GoToRed (B=8, clr=1) is terrible (-0.12)
- Adding second transition hurts (-6.88)

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Try p0x0 values: 0.97, 0.99, 1.0 (even higher)
- Try p1x0 values: 0.75, 0.70, 0.65, 0.80 (even lower)
- Try different LED colors
- Try swapping floor conditions: on white→GoToRed, on black→GoAwayGreen (different logic)

**Exploration:**
- Try 3-state: GoAwayGreen → on black → GoToRed → on white → GoAwayGreen (with extra state for gray)
- Try GoToColor(blue... wait blue=3 is bad) - no
- Try GoToColor(red=1) as S0 and GoAwayColor(green=2) as S1 with white/black triggers
- Try very high p0x0 close to 1.0

The key insight: p0x0=0.95 means 95% chance per step to switch when on black floor. The robot spends very little time on black before switching to GoToRed. p1x0=0.85 means 85% chance per step to switch when on white. The asymmetry (higher chance to leave black than white) seems optimal.

Let me try:
- p0x0: 0.97, 0.99, 0.95 (champion), 0.90
- p1x0: 0.75, 0.80, 0.85 (champion), 0.70, 0.65

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**
- New champion: p0x0=0.90, p1x0=0.80 scores -10.76
- Previous champion (p0x0=0.95, p1x0=0.85) scores -10.33
- p1x0=0.75 is consistently worse (~-9.3)
- The sweet spot seems to be around p0x0=0.90, p1x0=0.80

**Pattern emerging:**
- p0x0 (black floor → GoToRed): 0.90 is best so far (0.95 slightly worse, 0.99 worse)
- p1x0 (white floor → GoAwayGreen): 0.80 is best (0.85 slightly worse, 0.75 clearly worse)
- The 3-state variant (performer #4) also scores -10.33, suggesting 3-state doesn't hurt much

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Try p0x0 values near 0.90: 0.85, 0.88, 0.90, 0.92
- Try p1x0 values near 0.80: 0.78, 0.80, 0.82, 0.85
- Grid search around the optimum

**Exploration:**
- Try gray floor condition instead of black (C=1 instead of C=0) - transition when on GRAY floor
- Try white floor condition in S0 (transition when delivered)
- Try different color combinations

The fact that p0x0=0.90 beats p0x0=0.95 suggests: less eager to leave the source area. Maybe spending slightly more time at the source (picking up items more reliably) before heading to nest.

The fact that p1x0=0.80 beats p1x0=0.85 suggests: spending slightly more time at the nest before heading back. Maybe 0.75 is too little, 0.85 too much, 0.80 is the sweet spot.

Let me try a grid: p0x0 ∈ {0.85, 0.88, 0.90, 0.92, 0.95} × p1x0 ∈ {0.78, 0.80, 0.82, 0.85}

Also try:
- Gray floor trigger instead of black (C=1) for S0→S1
- 3-state with the champion parameters

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 9:**
- Champion unchanged: p0x0=0.90, p1x0=0.80 at -10.76 (but note this round's best was only -9.93)
- The champion parameters (0.90, 0.80) scored only -9.32 this round - high variance!
- Best this round: p0x0=0.85, p1x0=0.72 (-9.93); p0x0=0.90, p1x0=0.65 (-9.74)
- Lower p1x0 values (0.60-0.72) are competitive, not clearly better
- The scores are noisy - the champion's -10.76 may be a lucky run

**Analysis:**
- The champion (0.90, 0.80) has proven best over multiple rounds despite variance
- Going lower on p1x0 doesn't consistently improve things
- The landscape is flat and noisy around the optimum
- We should concentrate budget on re-running the champion and close variants to get reliable estimates

**Final round strategy:**
- Keep champion verbatim (elitism)
- Focus on the most promising region: p0x0 ∈ {0.88, 0.90, 0.92}, p1x0 ∈ {0.78, 0.80, 0.82}
- Also try some nearby variants that haven't been fully explored
- Include a few exploration candidates with different structure in case we missed something

**Key parameter combinations to try:**
- (0.90, 0.80) - champion
- (0.88, 0.80) - performer #5 from round 8
- (0.90, 0.78) - performer #3 from round 8

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 10 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.32 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
